Magnetic and antimagnetic rotational bands data tables
J. X. Teng

, K. Y. Ma

Yibin Research Institute, Jilin University, Sichuan 644000, China

Chongqing Research Institute, Jilin University, Chongqing, 400000, China
Abstract
The experimental results of 252 magnetic rotational bands reported in 123 nuclei and 38 antimagnetic rotational
bands reported in 27 nuclei are collected and listed in the present work, including energy, spin, parity, magnetic dipole
reduced transition probability B(M1), electric quadrupole reduced transition probability B(E2), B(M1)/B(E2) ratio,
and the ratio of the dynamic moment of inertia to the electric quadrupole reduced transition probability J 
(2)
/B(E2).
Following the presentation of the kinematic moment of inertia J 
(1)
, dynamic moment of inertia J 
(2)
, and I versus
rotational frequency ω, as well as energy staggering parameter S(I), B(M1), B(E2), B(M1)/B(E2), and J 
(2)
/B(E2)
versus I in A ∼ 60, 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions, a brief discussion is provided. Based on the systematic studies,
some nuclei are predicted to be candidates for magnetic or antimagnetic rotation.
∗
Corresponding author.
Email address: mky@jlu.edu.cn (K. Y. Ma)

1. Introduction
Magnetic rotation (MR) and antimagnetic rotation (AMR) are novel kinds of nuclear rotations occurring in weakly
deformed or nearly spherical nuclei, and have attracted enormous attention both experimentally and theoretically over
the past decades. For MR band [1], it is characterized by a ∆I=1 rotational structure with strongly enhanced M1 and
weak E2 transitions [2–4]. The explanation of such bands was given in terms of the shears mechanism predicted by
Frauendorf first [5]. In this mechanism, the angular momentum vectors of the high-j proton and neutron angular
momenta are nearly perpendicular to each other at the bandhead [6]. An increase in the energy and total angular
momentum is generated by the alignment of the proton and neutron angular momenta in the high-j orbitals. This
process can be regarded as the closing of two blades of a pair of shears. Thus, the MR band is also called the shears
band [3, 7]. A major feature of magnetic rotation is found under this interpretation: the component of the magnetic
dipole vector μ
⊥ 
is perpendicular to total angular momentum at the bandhead and decreases as the spin vectors align,
leading to the decrease of the magnetic dipole reduced transition probability B(M1) as the blades close.
The general properties of magnetic rotation are summarized as [2–4, 8, 9]:
(1) The states in the MR bands generally follow a rotational-like behavior with the energies following the pattern of
E(I) − E
0 
∼ A(I − I
0
)
2
, where I is the spin of the state, I
0 
is the spin of the bandhead and A is constant;
(2) The magnetic dipole reduced transition probability B(M1) value is large (∼ 2 − 10 μ
2
N 
) and decreases.
(3) The electric quadrupole (E2) transitions are weak or unobserved, and values of electric quadrupole reduced
transition probability B(E2) are very small. The B(M1)/B(E2) ratio values are large (& 20 [μ
N 
/(eb)]
2
);
(4) The quadrupole deformation parameter is small and less than 0.15 in most cases;
(5) The ratio of the dynamic moment of inertia J 
(2) 
to the B(E2) values are large (¿100 MeV
−1
(eb)
−2
) compared
with the values in well deformed (∼ 10 MeV
−1
(eb)
−2
) or superdeformed bands (∼ 5 MeV
−1
(eb)
−2
).
MR bands have been investigated theoretically using various models including shell model [10] and the many-
particles-plus-rotor models [11] and the titled axis cranking (TAC) models [5] have been proved to be the powerful
tools. The validity of TAC approximation was discussed and tested in comparison with particle-rotor model [12]. The
Skyrme-Hartree-Fock Method with the TAC has been used to investigate MR band in 
142
Gd [13]. Because of the high
numerical complexity of the TAC model, most of the applications are based on simple phenomenological Hamiltonians,
such as the pairing-plus-quadrupole-quadrupole tilted-axis cranking (PQTAC) model [14]. The covariant density func-
tional theory (CDFT) has received a lot of attention during the recent years. In 2000, the three-dimensional cranking
CDFT was established by Madokoro et al [15]. However, it has only been used to investigate magnetic rotation in 
84
Rb.
In comparison, the two-dimensional cranking CDFT reduces the computing time considerably, and thus can be applied
for medium heavy nucleus such as 
142
Gd [16]. Moreover, the TAC model based on the CDFT with the point-coupling
interaction was developed by Zhao et al [17], which has been successfully applied for MR ranging from light nuclei
58
Fe [18], 
60
Ni [17] to heavy nuclei 
198, 199
Pb [19].
For AMR band [3, 8], it is characterized by a ∆I=2 rotational structure with a rather regular cascade of weak E2
transitions. The energy and higher angular momentum are obtained by the ‘two-shears-like mechanism’, which was
first proposed by Frauendorf [3]. In this mechanism, the angular momenta of protons (neutrons) are aligned back to
back in opposite directions and roughly perpendicular to the orientation of the total angular momentum of neutrons
(protons) at the bandhead. Along this band, energy and angular momentum are increased by simultaneous closing of
3

the two blades of protons and neutrons toward the the neutron (proton) angular momentum vector [8, 20]. Under this
interpretation, the transverse magnetic moment is zero since μ
⊥ 
of the two subsystems are antialigned and cancel each
other.
The experimental indicators for AMR bands can be summarized as:
(1) A ∆I=2 sequence with E2 transitions only, as the cancellation of the magnetic moments leads to the absence of
the M1 transition;
(2) The structures are weakly deformed leading to small B(E2) values, which decrease with the increasing spin;
(3) The ratio of the dynamic moment of inertia to electric quadrupole reduced transition probability J 
(2)
/B(E2)
values are large (¿ 100 ¯h
2
MeV
−1
(eb)
−2
).
From the theoretical aspect, antimagnetic rotation has been discussed mainly by the semiclassical particle rotor
model [2] and TAC model [16, 17, 21]. Many investigations in the framework of microscopic-macroscopic model [22–
24], pairing plus quadrupole model [3, 25], and the CDFT [20, 26–30] with the point coupling effective interaction
PC-PK1 [31] have been carried out based on the TAC model. The cranked shell model (CSM) with pairing correlations
treated by a particle-number-conserving (PNC) method [32, 33], is also successfully used to investigate AMR bands.
Both magnetic and antimagnetic rotations have been investigated in experimental and theoretical research for many
years. Since the clear evidence for shears mechanism has first been provided through lifetime measurements for four
M1 bands in 
198, 199
Pb [34], more and more nuclei are confirmed to have MR or AMR bands. We have expanded the
previous compilation of MR bands made by Amita et al. in 2000 [35]. Up to now, 252 magnetic rotational bands in
123 nuclei have been reported in the A ∼ 60 [17, 18, 36–39], 80 [40–81], 110 [25, 29, 82–140], 140 [13, 141–211], and
190 [6, 7, 34, 212–272] mass regions. In contrast, the experimental observation of AMR bands is scarce and so far only
38 antimagnetic rotational bands are reported in 27 nuclei and mainly distributed in the A ∼ 60 [18, 39, 273], 110
[20, 22–25, 28–30, 107, 109, 116, 127, 274–306], and 140 [153, 211, 307–311] mass regions. The nuclei of magnetic and
antimagnetic rotations in the nuclear chart are given in Fig. 1.

Fig. 1: (color online) On the nuclear chart, the nuclides with magnetic rotation and antimagnetic rotation are observed. The blue squares represent
the nuclei of magnetic rotation only. The red squares represent the nuclei of antimagnetic rotation only. The purple squares represent the coexistence
of both magnetic and antimagnetic rotations. The light blue background represents the currently observable nuclei.
The numbers of observed MR and AMR bands as a function of neutron and proton numbers, respectively, are shown
in Fig. 2. Most MR bands are reported near magic or semi-magic numbers N = 107−120, 74−83, 54−66, 42−48, and
32−34. Same is also true for protons where MR bands are seen near Z = 80−83, 55−64, 45−51, 35−40, and 26−28,
among which the Pb isotopes (Z = 82) have the largest number of MR bands reported. A majority of AMR bands are
reported near numbers N = 77−80, 54−64, 32, and 33. The largest number of AMR bands are reported in N = 60. In
terms of proton number, AMR bands are reported at Z = 26, 28, 46, 48, 49, 54, 56, 60, 63, and 66.
5

Fig. 2: Numbers of MR and AMR bands as a function of neutron and proton number, respectively
In order to promote the study of magnetic and antimagnetic rotations, the compilation of the data for MR and AMR
bands is demanded. In this paper, we mainly focus on the discussion of spin I, kinematic moment of inertia J 
(1)
,
and dynamic moment of inertia J 
(2) 
versus rotational frequency ω, as well as energy staggering parameter S(I), the
magnetic dipole reduced transition probability B(M1), the electric quadrupole reduced transition probability B(E2)
and the B(M1)/B(E2) ratio versus spin I in A ∼ 60, 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions for MR bands. Moreover, the
spin I, kinematic moment of inertia J 
(1)
, and dynamic moment of inertia J 
(2) 
versus rotational frequency ω, as well
as the electric quadrupole reduced transition probability B(E2) and the J 
(2)
/B(E2) ratio versus spin I for AMR bands
in A ∼ 60, 110, and 140 mass regions are also discussed. Experimental data on MR and AMR bands are presented in
Table A and B, respectively.
2. Systematics of magnetic rotational bands
2.1. The spin vs. rotational frequency
Figs. 3 to 6 show the relations between spins and rotational frequencies for all magnetic rotational bands in A ∼
60, 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions, respectively. It is noted that a number of MR bands are observed to display
band-crossing phenomenon in A ∼ 110, 140, and 190 mass regions from Figs. 4 to 6. However, as shown in Fig. 3,
the majority of nuclei do not show the band-crossing phenomenon in A ∼ 60 and 80 mass regions. Generally, the
spin tends to grow smoothly with increasing rotational frequency before and after the backbending. But, there is an
6

abnormal phenomenon in 
83
Rb and 
135
Te, where the spin decreases with rotational frequency increasing. Moveover,
the magnetic rotation occurs at the lowest value spin 5.5¯h for 
83
Rb, and the highest value spin 37.5¯h for 
193
Hg.

2.2. The kinematic moment of inertia vs. rotational frequency
The kinematic moments of inertia for all magnetic rotational bands in A ∼ 60, 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions
are given in Figs. 7 to 10, respectively. The value of J 
(1) 
is estimated by using the relation J 
(1)
=I/ω. It is clear
that the kinematic moment of inertia gradually decreases with the rotational frequency increasing before and after the
backbending, and the decline speed gradually becomes gentler as the rotational frequency increases. However, one
observes an opposite trend for MR bands in 
86
Sr, 
87
Zr, and 
144
Tb where J 
(1) 
generally rises with increasing rotational
frequency. It can be seen that in the A ∼ 60 mass region, J 
(1) 
varies from 9 to 31 keV/¯h, in the A ∼ 80 mass region,
J 
(1) 
varies from 9 to 57 keV/¯h except for 
90
Zr, and it varies from 17 to 85 keV/¯h in the A ∼ 110 mass region. Then, in
the A ∼ 140 mass region, J 
(1) 
varies from 20 to 154 keV/¯h, and finally it varies from 39 to 258 keV/¯h in the A ∼ 190
mass region. It is shown that both the upper and lower bounds of the J 
(1) 
values gradually increase with the change
of the mass region from A ∼ 60 to A ∼ 190 mass region.

2.3. The dynamic moment of inertia vs. rotational frequency
Figs. 11 to 14 show the dynamic moments of inertia J 
(2) 
for all magnetic rotational bands in A ∼ 60, 80, 110, 140, and
190 mass regions, respectively. The value of J 
(2) 
is estimated by using the relation J 
(2)
=1/[E
γ
(I + 1 → I) − E
γ
(I →
I − 1)]. A large fluctuation of J 
(2) 
values exists in A ∼ 60, 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions since the J 
(2) 
corresponds
to the second derivative of the energy with the spin. Furthermore, it is clear that J 
(2) 
values of MR bands are quite
small and some of them are roughly constant while others have a large fluctuation with rotational frequency increasing.

Fig. 14: (Color online) Dynamic moment of inertia versus rotational frequency for magnetic rotational bands in A ∼ 190 mass region.
2.4. The energy staggering vs. spin
Figs. 15 to 18 show the plots of S(I) vs. I for all magnetic rotational bands in A ∼ 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions,
respectively. No signature splitting should be observed in ideal MR bands due to the pure individual motion of nucleons
[3, 5, 312]. However, the phenomena of signature splitting are shown in many bands, such as in 
105, 109
Ag, 
102, 104
Cd,
135
Ce, 
142
Eu, 
144
Dy, 
193, 195, 196
Hg, and 
198
Bi (No judgement on the presence or absence of splitting could however be
made in many cases where only 4−5 levels have been observed.) Moreover, it can be seen that S(I) in most MR bands
increase as the spin increasing except for the band-crossing regions. But S(I) shows decreasing trend as a function of
spin in 
83, 85
Rb, 
86
Sr, 
135
Te, 
133
Pr, 
134
Nd, 
144
Gd, 
191
Pb and 
198
Bi. In addition, we can infer from J 
(1)
(= 1/[2S(I)]) that
both the upper and lower bounds of the S(I) values decrease with the change of the mass region from A ∼ 60 to A ∼
190.

2.6. The electric quadrupole reduced transition probability vs. spin
Figs. 23 to 26 show the electric quadrupole reduced transition probabilities B(E2) for magnetic rotational bands in
A ∼ 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions, respectively. It is well known that the values of B(E2) are small, B(E2) ∼ 0.1
(eb)
2 
[2]. Indeed, most nuclei clearly satisfy the criterion. However, the values of B(E2) are less than 0.1 (eb)
2 
in A ∼ 80
mass region, while the values of B(E2) vary between 0.5−1.0 (eb)
2 
in 
112
Sb. Furthermore, B(E2) decreases as the spin
increases except for 
108
In, 
128
Ba, 
141
Eu, and 
142
Gd. B(E2) values also show a large staggering in the MR bands of 
107
Ag,
137
Nd, and 
192
Hg.

Up to now, the MR bands have been shown in 64 odd-A nuclei, 25 odd-odd nuclei, and 34 even-even nuclei. It is
obvious that the MR bands are reported in the odd-odd isotopes of Sb and Tb as well as the odd-A isotopes of Br, Kr,
La, and Pr. According to the systematics of magnetic rotation, we may infer that 
80

Pd shows a significant splitting phenomenon.

It is well known that AMR bands are expected to be observed in the same regions as MR bands [3]. Up to now, AMR
bands are observed in A ∼ 60, 110, and 140 mass regions, while MR bands are observed not only in the mass regions of
A ∼ 60, 110, and 140 but also in A ∼ 80 and A ∼ 190 mass regions. As a result, we can deduce that AMR bands exist in
29

the A ∼ 80 and A ∼ 190 mass regions as well, which can be investigated further experimentally. In addition, according
to the systematics of antimagnetic rotation, we may predict that 
111
In could be a candidate nucleus with AMR bands.
Then, it is interesting to note that there exist multiple AMR bands in 
58
Fe, 
103, 105
Pd, 
107, 109
Cd, and 
108, 109, 110
In.
30

4. Summary
In this paper, 252 magnetic rotational bands reported in 123 nuclei and 38 antimagnetic rotational bands reported
in 27 nuclei are collected and listed. Following the presentation of the kinematic moment of inertia J 
(1)
, dynamic
moment of inertia J 
(2)
, and I versus rotational frequency ω, as well as energy staggering parameter S(I), B(M1),
B(E2), B(M1)/B(E2), and J 
(2)
/B(E2) versus I in A ∼ 60, 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions, the main features of
magnetic and antimagnetic rotational bands are also discussed in detail.
For magnetic rotation, lots of bands are observed to show the phenomena of band-crossing and signature splitting.
The kinematic moment of inertia J 
(1) 
gradually decreases with the rotational frequency increasing. The regularity of
S(I) in A ∼ 60, 80, 110, 140, and 190 mass regions is contrary to J 
(1)
, which is also well confirmed from the research
in this paper. Furthermore, the values of B(M1) are large while the values of B(E2) are small (There is an exception
for 
112
Sb whose values range between 0.5−1.0 (eb)
2
). Both values decrease with increasing spin. The B(M1)/B(E2)
values are generally more than 10 [μ
N 
/(eb)]
2
. According to the systematics of magnetic rotation, we may infer that
80
Rb, 
108
Ag, 
114
Sb, 
137
Ce, 
135, 140
Nd, 
140
Sm, 
190
Pb, and 
196, 201
Bi could be the candidate nuclei with magnetic rotational
bands.
For antimagnetic rotation, multiple antimagnetic rotational bands in 
58
Fe, 
103, 105
Pd, 
107, 109
Cd, 
108, 109, 110
In, and
143
Eu are shown. The spin generally grows steadily as the rotational frequency increases. The kinematic moment
of inertia J 
(1) 
decreases with the increasing rotational frequency. In addition, the values of B(E2) are small, less
than 0.4 (eb)
2
. The J 
(2)
/B(E2) roughly grows with the spin increasing, and J 
(2)
/B(E2) values should exceed 100
¯h
2
MeV
−1
(eb)
−2
. The systematic study of antimagnetic rotation suggests that the 
111
In may be a candidate nucleus with
antimagnetic rotational bands, and A ∼ 80 and A ∼ 190 mass regions could be predicted as the candidate antimagnetic
rotational mass regions.

Table 1. Magnetic and antimagnetic rotational bands
A
Z 
X
N 
Denotes the specific nuclide with
X Element symbol
A Mass number
Z Proton number
N Neutron number
E Level energy in units of keV. Energies in parentheses denote tentative levels. Labels X, Y, Z, etc.,
indicate that absolute excitation energies are unknown due to lack of knowledge about linking
transitions to the lower levels.
I
π 
I denotes the level spin for each band member. π denotes the parity (+ or −). I
π 
given in paren-
theses denotes uncertain spin and/or parity assignments.
E
γ
(M1) γ-Ray energies in units of keV for the M1 (∆I=1) transition I→I−1.
E
γ
(E2) γ-Ray energies in units of keV for the E2 (∆I=2) transition I→I−2.
ω The rotational frequencies in units of MeV from the definition ¯hω(I)=E(I) − E(I − 1) in magnetic
rotation and the definition ¯hω(I)=[E(I) − E(I − 2)]/2 in antimagnetic rotation.
J 
(1)

2

(1)

2
/MeV from the definition J 
(2)
=dI/dω.
S(I) The energy staggering parameters in units of ¯h/MeV from the definition S(I)=[E(I)−E(I − 1)]/2I.
B(M1) The magnetic dipole reduced transition probability in units of μ

given with the uncertainties in
parentheses. The number of digits corresponds to the original experimental article.
